Remark 4.1
.

The function
x\mapsto[x]
takes real values if
\tau

is purely imaginary and
L>0
, and its restriction to the interval

0<x<L
is positive. There is then an open subset of

\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, which includes the affine alcove of the next
section, on which the arguments of the square roots are positive,
and where the square roots are defined to be positive. On this
domain the star-triangle relation is verified. The Boltzmann
weights have then an analytic continuation to 2-valued functions on
the complexification
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
. The star-triangle relation
continues to hold by analytic continuation.

4.2.
Restricted model: the setting

Let
\mathfrak{g}
be one of the classical Lie algebras
A_{n-1}

(
n\geq 2
),
B_{n}
(
n\geq 2
),
C_{n}
(
n\geq 1
) or
D_{n}

(
n\geq 3
) and let
g=n,2n-1,n+1,2n
, respectively, be their dual
Coxeter numbers. Let
\alpha_{i}\in\mathfrak{h}^{*}
be a system of roots and
\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\in\mathfrak{h}
the
corresponding coroots. Denote by
\theta
the highest root and
\rho
the
half-sum of positive roots. The crossing parameter is

\lambda=-g(\theta,\theta)/4.

(25)

In the restricted model the dynamical
variables are in the subset
P_{++}^{\ell+g}
of the weight lattice
P
consisting of regular dominant affine weights of level

\ell+g
, namely

P_{++}^{\ell+g}=\{a\in P\,|\,\langle a,\alpha_{i}^{\vee}\rangle>0,\quad\langle a,\theta^{\vee}\rangle<\ell+g\},

where
\theta^{\vee}
is the coroot corresponding to
\theta
.
They are shifts by
\rho
of the classical part of the dominant weights of level
\ell

of the corresponding untwisted affine Kac–Moody algebra

\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}
, which are highest weights of integrable representations.
The parameter
L
is

L=\frac{(\theta,\theta)}{2}(\ell+g)

(26)

It is an integer (even in the
C_{n}
case) such that
L+2\lambda\geq 0
.
In terms of the invariant inner product
(\ ,\ )
on
\mathfrak{h}^{*}
, the inequalities defining
P_{++}^{\ell+g}
are

(a,\alpha_{i})>0,\quad(a,\theta)<L.

Explicitly,

\displaystyle P_{++}^{\ell+g}=\begin{cases}\{a\in P_{++}\,|\,a_{1}-a_{n}<L\}&\textrm{ for }A_{n-1}^{(1)},\\
\{a\in P_{++}\,|\,a_{1}+a_{2}<L\}&\textrm{ for }B_{n}^{(1)},\\
\{a\in P_{++}\,|\,a_{1}<L/2\}&\textrm{ for }C_{n}^{(1)},\\
\{a\in P_{++}\,|\,a_{1}+a_{2}<L\}&\textrm{ for }D_{n}^{(1)},\end{cases}

where
P_{++}
is the set of regular dominant weights,

\displaystyle P_{++}=\begin{cases}\{a\in P\,|\,a_{1}>a_{2}>\cdots>a_{n}\}&A_{n-1},\\
\{a\in P\,|\,a_{1}>a_{2}>\cdots>a_{n},a_{n}>0\}&B_{n},\\
\{a\in P\,|\,a_{1}>a_{2}>\cdots>a_{n},a_{n}>0\}&C_{n},\\
\{a\in P\,|\,a_{1}>a_{2}>\cdots>a_{n},a_{n-1}+a_{n}>0\}&D_{n},\\
\end{cases}

and
P
is the weight lattice,

\displaystyle P=\begin{cases}\{a\in\left(\tfrac{1}{n}\mathbb{Z}\right)^{n}\,|\,\forall i,j:a_{i}-a_{j}\in\mathbb{Z},\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=0\}&A_{n-1},\\
\{a\in\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\right)^{n}\,|\,\forall i,j:a_{i}-a_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}\}&B_{n},\\
\{a\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}\}&C_{n},\\
\{a\in\left(\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}\right)^{n}\,|\,\forall i,j:a_{i}-a_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}\}&D_{n}.\\
\end{cases}

Let
\pi
be the full subgroupoid of the transformation groupoid
P\rtimes P
on
P^{\ell+g}_{++}
. It has
P_{++}^{\ell+g}
as its set of objects, whereas its set of morphisms consists of pairs
(a,\mu)
of weights, with the source
a
, the target
a+\mu
, such that
a,a+\mu\in P^{\ell+g}_{++}
, and the composition law
(a_{1},\mu_{1})\circ(a_{2},\mu_{2})=(a_{2},\mu_{1}+\mu_{2})
if
a_{1}=a_{2}+\mu_{2}
.

We consider the
\pi
-graded space
V^{\textrm{RSOS}}=\bigoplus_{(a,\mu)\in\pi}V^{\textrm{RSOS}}_{(a,\mu)}
with

\displaystyle V^{\textrm{RSOS}}_{(a,\mu)}=\begin{cases}\bar{V}_{\varepsilon_{i}}=\mathbb{C}e_{i}&\text{if $\mu=\varepsilon_{i}$, $i\neq 0$, and $a,a+\varepsilon_{i}\in P^{\ell+g}_{++}$,}\\
\bar{V}_{0}=\mathbb{C}e_{0}&\text{in the $B_{n}^{(1)}$ case if $\mu=\varepsilon_{0}$, $a\in P^{\ell+g}_{++}$ and $a_{n}\neq\frac{1}{2}$,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

In the
A_{n-1}^{(1)}
case we also act on the dual space, whose restriction
(V^{\ast})^{\textrm{RSOS}}=\bigoplus_{(a,\mu)\in\pi}(V^{\ast})^{\textrm{RSOS}}_{(a,\mu)}
is

\displaystyle(V^{*})^{\textrm{RSOS}}_{(a,\mu)}=\begin{cases}\bar{V}_{\varepsilon_{-i}}=\mathbb{C}e_{-i}&\text{if $\mu=\varepsilon_{-i}$ and $a,a+\varepsilon_{-i}\in P^{l}_{++}$,}\\
0&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

where
(e_{-1},...,e_{-n})
denotes the basis of
\bar{V}^{*}
dual to the basis
(e_{1},\dots,e_{n})
of
\bar{V}
.

4.3.
Restricted
R
-matrix and rotations

We define the restricted
R
-matrix to be the one given in Section
4.1
, such that the matrix elements
\check{R}_{i,j}^{k,l}(u)
now become homomorphisms of
\pi
-graded vector spaces through the action of
E_{ki}\otimes E_{lj}
that sends
V^{\textrm{RSOS}}_{(a,\varepsilon_{i})}\otimes V^{\textrm{RSOS}}_{(a+\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j})}
to
V^{\textrm{RSOS}}_{(a,\varepsilon_{k})}\otimes V^{\textrm{RSOS}}_{(a+\varepsilon_{k},\varepsilon_{l})}
.

Let us give the inner product
\omega:V^{\textrm{RSOS}}_{(a,\varepsilon_{i})}\otimes V^{\textrm{RSOS}}_{(a+\varepsilon_{i},-\varepsilon_{i})}\to\mathbb{C}
and its dual map
\sigma:\mathbb{C}\to V^{\textrm{RSOS}}_{(a,\varepsilon_{i})}\otimes V^{\textrm{RSOS}}_{(a+\varepsilon_{i},-\varepsilon_{i})}
in
B_{n}^{(1)}
,
C_{n}^{(1)}
and
D_{n}^{(1)}
cases as

\displaystyle\omega(e_{i}\otimes_{v}e_{-j})

\displaystyle:=\delta_{\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j}}\displaystyle\sqrt{\sigma\frac{G_{t((a,\varepsilon_{i}))}}{G_{s((a,\varepsilon_{i}))}}},\quad\sigma(1):=\sum_{k}e_{-k}\otimes_{v}e_{k}\displaystyle\sqrt{\sigma\frac{G_{t((a,\varepsilon_{-k}))}}{G_{s((a,\varepsilon_{-k}))}}},

(27)

where
\sigma
in the argument of the square root is
1
for
B^{(1)}_{n},D^{(1)}_{n}
and
-1
for
C^{(1)}_{n}
, see Section
4.1
.
In the case of algebra
A_{n-1}^{(1)}
, we set the maps
\omega
,
\sigma
,
\omega^{*}
and
\sigma^{*}
to be

\displaystyle\omega(e_{i}\otimes_{v}e_{-j})

\displaystyle=\delta_{\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j}}\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{G_{t((a,\varepsilon_{i}))}}{G_{s((a,\varepsilon_{i}))}}},\qquad\sigma(1)=\sum_{k}e_{-k}\otimes_{v}e_{k}\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{G_{t((a,-\varepsilon_{k}))}}{G_{s((a,-\varepsilon_{k}))}}}

(28)

\displaystyle\omega^{*}(e_{-i}\otimes_{v}e_{j})

\displaystyle=\delta_{\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j}}\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{G_{t((a,-\varepsilon_{i}))}}{G_{s((a,-\varepsilon_{i}))}}},\qquad\sigma^{*}(1)=\sum_{k}e_{k}\otimes_{v}e_{-k}\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{G_{t((a,\varepsilon_{k}))}}{G_{s((a,\varepsilon_{k}))}}}.

Here,
s
and
t
are the source and target maps, respectively, with
s((a,\varepsilon_{i}))=a
and
t((a,\varepsilon_{i}))=a+\varepsilon_{i}
.

The square roots are defined by analytic continuation from the region where their arguments are positive, as in Remark
4.1
.

We now want to obtain the rotations of the
A_{n-1}^{(1)}
-type
R
-matrix
\check{R}_{VV}(u)
using Definition
3.5
. The rotation by
-90^{\circ}
is

\check{R}_{V^{*}V}(u,a):=\sum_{-\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}=\varepsilon_{k}-\varepsilon_{l}}(\check{R}_{V^{*}V})^{k,-l}_{-i,j}(u,a)E_{k,-i}\otimes E_{-l,j},

(29)

with

(\check{R}_{V^{*}V})^{k,-l}_{-i,j}(u,a):=\sqrt{\frac{G_{a+\varepsilon_{k}}G_{a-\varepsilon_{i}}}{G_{a}G_{a-\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}}}}(\check{R}_{VV})_{j,l}^{i,k}(\lambda-u,a-\varepsilon_{i}),

graphically presented as
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}_{V^{*}V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{j}}

:=\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{G_{b}G_{d}}{G_{a}G_{c}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}_{VV}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{l}}
.

The rotation by
90^{\circ}
is

\check{R}_{VV^{*}}(u,a):=\sum_{\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}=-\varepsilon_{k}+\varepsilon_{l}}(\check{R}_{VV^{*}})^{-k,l}_{i,-j}(u,a)E_{-k,i}\otimes E_{l,-j},

(30)

with

\displaystyle(\check{R}_{VV^{*}})^{-k,l}_{i,-j}(u,a):=\sqrt{\frac{G_{a-\varepsilon_{k}}G_{a+\varepsilon_{i}}}{G_{a}G_{a+\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}}}}(\check{R}_{VV})_{j,l}^{i,k}(\lambda-u,a-\varepsilon_{k}),

depicted as
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}_{VV^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{j}}

:=\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{G_{b}G_{d}}{G_{a}G_{c}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}_{VV}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{i}}
.

Finally, the rotation by
180^{\circ}
is

\check{R}_{V^{*}V^{*}}(u,a):=\sum_{\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}=\varepsilon_{k}+\varepsilon_{l}}(\check{R}_{V^{*}V^{*}})^{-k,-l}_{-i,-j}(u,a)E_{-k,-i}\otimes E_{-l,-j},

(31)

with

(\check{R}_{V^{*}V^{*}})^{-k,-l}_{-i,-j}(u,a):=(\check{R}_{VV})^{j,i}_{l,k}(u,a-\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}),

which corresponds graphically to
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}_{V^{*}V^{*}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{j}}

:=

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}_{VV}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{k}}
.

4.4.
Restricted model: results

The vector space
V^{\textrm{RSOS}}
is constructed in such a way that allows only the
admissible pairs of weights
, as defined in
[
30
]
. Therefore, the condition that the
R
-matrices
\check{R}_{VV}(u)
and
\check{R}(u)
are well-defined on the
V^{\textrm{RSOS}}\otimes V^{\textrm{RSOS}}
is equivalent to the
finiteness property
of the restricted Jimbo–Miwa–Okado Boltzmann weights. It was shown that the restricted weights are finite and satisfy the star-triangle relation (
20
) among themselves, which translates into the following result

Lemma 4.2
.

The
\check{R}_{VV}(u)
in the
A_{n-1}^{(1)}
case and
\check{R}(u)
in
B_{n}^{(1)}
,
C_{n}^{(1)}
,
D_{n}^{(1)}
cases are well-defined on
V^{\mathrm{RSOS}}\otimes V^{\mathrm{RSOS}}
and their restrictions to

V^{\mathrm{RSOS}}\otimes V^{\mathrm{RSOS}}
satisfy the Yang–Baxter
equation. Furthermore, they satisfy the inversion relations
\check{R}_{VV}(u)\check{R}_{VV}(-u)=\mathbb{1}
and
\check{R}(u)\check{R}(-u)=\mathbb{1}
on
V^{\mathrm{RSOS}}\otimes V^{\mathrm{RSOS}}
, where
\mathbb{1}
is an identity matrix depending on the algebra type.

Proof.

The Yang–Baxter equations follow directly from the definition of
\check{R}(u)
,
\check{R}_{VV}(u)
respectively, and the star-triangle relation (
20
). The inversion relations in the non-restricted case follow from relation (
58
),

\displaystyle\check{R}(u)\check{R}(-u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}=\varepsilon_{k}+\varepsilon_{l},\\
\varepsilon_{m}+\varepsilon_{o}=\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}\end{subarray}}\check{R}^{k,l}_{i,j}(u)\check{R}^{i,j}_{m,o}(-u)E_{km}\otimes E_{lo}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}=\varepsilon_{k}+\varepsilon_{l},\\
\varepsilon_{m}+\varepsilon_{o}=\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{W}\Big(\begin{matrix}b&a\\
g&c\end{matrix}\Big|u\Big)\mathcal{W}\Big(\begin{matrix}b&g\\
d&c\end{matrix}\Big|-u\Big)E_{km}\otimes E_{lo}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}=\varepsilon_{k}+\varepsilon_{l},\\
\varepsilon_{m}+\varepsilon_{o}=\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}\end{subarray}}\delta_{k,m}\delta_{l,o}E_{km}\otimes E_{lo}=\sum_{\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}=\varepsilon_{l}-\varepsilon_{k}}E_{kk}\otimes E_{ll}=\mathbb{1},

where the dynamical parameters
a,b,c,d,g
are as in the picture:

\sum_{k,l,m,o}\sum_{g}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{j}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}(-u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{o}}
=\mathbb{1}
.

To see that this relation holds on the restricted space, it is enough to use the argument proved in
[
30
]
, that the weight

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{t}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{u}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{s}}
with
(a,\varepsilon_{t}),(d,\varepsilon_{s})\in\pi
and
(a,\varepsilon_{r})\notin\pi
vanishes. The action of the inversion relation on
V^{\mathrm{RSOS}}\otimes V^{\mathrm{RSOS}}
means that
(b,\varepsilon_{m}),(d,\varepsilon_{o})\in\pi
and therefore
\check{R}_{m,o}^{i,j}(-u)
vanishes unless
(b,\varepsilon_{i})
and
(g,\varepsilon_{j})
are inside
\pi
. Since
(b,\varepsilon_{i}),(g,\varepsilon_{j})
have to be in
\pi
, the same reasoning holds for the component
\check{R}_{i,j}^{k,l}(u)
, showing that the terms in the inversion relation with arrows outside the groupoid
\pi
vanish. Thus the relation holds on the restricted space.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

The operators
\check{R}_{V^{*}V}(u)
,
\check{R}_{VV^{*}}(u)
and
\check{R}_{V^{*}V^{*}}(u)
in the case
A_{n-1}^{(1)}
are well-defined on restricted spaces and satisfy the relations

\begin{split}&\check{R}^{(23)}_{V^{*}V}(u_{1}-u_{2})\check{R}^{(12)}_{V^{*}V}(u_{1})\check{R}^{(23)}_{VV}(u_{2})=\check{R}^{(12)}_{VV}(u_{2})\check{R}^{(23)}_{V^{*}V}(u_{1})\check{R}^{(12)}_{V^{*}V}(u_{1}-u_{2}),\end{split}

(32)

\begin{split}\check{R}^{(23)}_{V^{*}V^{*}}(u_{1}-u_{2})\check{R}^{(12)}_{V^{*}V}(u_{1})\check{R}^{(23)}_{V^{*}V}(u_{2})=\check{R}^{(12)}_{V^{*}V}(u_{2})\check{R}^{(23)}_{V^{*}V}(u_{1})\check{R}^{(12)}_{V^{*}V^{*}}(u_{1}-u_{2}).\end{split}

(33)

on restricted spaces.

Proof.

The operators
\check{R}_{V^{*}V}(u)
,
\check{R}_{VV^{*}}(u)
and
\check{R}_{V^{*}V^{*}}(u)
are well-defined on restricted spaces since they are given by well-defined maps
\omega
,
\omega^{*}
,
\sigma
,
\sigma^{*}
and
\check{R}_{VV}(u)
. The relations (
32
) and (
33
) follow from the Lemma
3.8
.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

The restriction of
\check{R}(u)
to
V^{\mathrm{RSOS}}\otimes V^{\mathrm{RSOS}}
is crossing symmetric, i.e. it obeys the relations

\displaystyle\omega^{(23)}\check{R}^{(12)}(u+\lambda)\check{R}^{(23)}(u)=\rho(u)\omega\otimes\mathbb{1},

\displaystyle\check{R}^{(12)}(u)\check{R}^{(23)}(u+\lambda)\sigma^{(12)}=\rho(u)\mathbb{1}\otimes\sigma,

for
B_{n}^{(1)}
,
C_{n}^{(1)}
,
D_{n}^{(1)}
, where
\mathbb{1}
is the
(2n+1)\times(2n+1)
identity matrix in
B_{n}^{(1)}
case and
2n\times 2n
identity matrix in
C_{n}^{(1)}
and
D_{n}^{(1)}
cases. The function
\rho
is

\rho(u)=\frac{[u-1][\lambda+u]}{[u][1+\lambda+u]}.

(34)

Proof.

For the
R
-matrix (
22
) we have

\displaystyle\left(\omega^{(23)}\check{R}^{(12)}(u+\lambda)\check{R}^{(23)}(u)-\rho(u)\omega^{(12)}\otimes\mathbb{1}\right)e_{i}\otimes e_{j}\otimes e_{k}

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\sum_{g}\sqrt{\frac{G_{g}}{G_{a+\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}+\varepsilon_{k}}}}W\Big(\begin{matrix}a&a+\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}+\varepsilon_{k}\\
a+\varepsilon_{i}&g\end{matrix}\Big|u+\lambda\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad W\Big(\begin{matrix}a+\varepsilon_{i}&g\\
a+\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}&a+\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}+\varepsilon_{k}\end{matrix}\Big|u\Big)-\sqrt{\frac{G_{a+\varepsilon_{i}}}{G_{a}}}\delta_{0,\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}}\rho(u)\Bigg)e_{i+j+k}=0

which follows from the equation (
62
). Here
e_{i+j+k}
is the vector in the weight space
\bar{V}_{\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j}+\varepsilon_{k}}
. The second relation can be verified in a similar way using the identity (
63
).

Now we have to check that the contributions of the arrows that are not elements of the subgroupoid
\pi
and the contributions where
V_{(a,\varepsilon_{0})}=\mathbb{C}e_{0}
for
a_{n}=\frac{1}{2}
appears, vanish. Suppose that
(a,\varepsilon_{i})
,
(a,\varepsilon_{k})
,
(b,\varepsilon_{l})
,
(d,\varepsilon_{j})
are composable arrows in the subgroupoid
\pi
such that none of them satisfies the condition that the
n
-th component of the local state is
\frac{1}{2}
while the arrow is
\varepsilon_{0}
. Now assume that
(b,\varepsilon_{p})\not\in\pi
or that
(b,\varepsilon_{p})=(b,\varepsilon_{0})
with
b\in P^{\ell+g}_{++}
.

\sum_{g}\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{G_{g}}{G_{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{j}}
=\delta_{a,d}\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{G_{b}}{G_{a}}}.

As shown in Theorem 7 in
[
30
]
, in that case
\check{R}^{p,-m}_{l,j}(b,u)=0
, unless
\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}=B_{n}^{(1)}
,
b_{n}=\frac{1}{2}
and
p=0
or
p=-n
. The only configurations compatible with the first case
p=0
are
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{l}}

=\check{R}^{k,0}_{k,0}(u+\lambda,a)\check{R}^{0,0}_{l,-l}(u,b).

Here
k\neq 0,n
and
l\neq 0,-n
because otherwise the arrows
(a,\varepsilon_{k})
and
(b,\varepsilon_{l})
are not in the subgroupoid
\pi
or the arrows connected to
b
should be
0
, which contradicts the assumption.

Similarly, in the second case
p=-n
the only non-vanishing configurations are
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{l}}

=\check{R}^{k,-n}_{k,-n}(u+\lambda,a)\check{R}^{-n,n}_{l,-l}(u,b)

with
k\neq 0,n
and
l\neq 0,-n
for the same reason as above. By direct computation one can show that

\displaystyle\check{R}^{k,0}_{k,0}(u+\lambda,a)\check{R}^{0,0}_{l,-l}(u,b)\sqrt{G_{b}}+\check{R}^{k,-n}_{k,-n}(u+\lambda,a)\check{R}^{-n,n}_{l,-l}(u,b)\sqrt{G_{b-\varepsilon_{n}}}=0.

The only thing left to prove is that the weight

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{r}}
=\check{R}^{i,0}_{p,r}(u,a)

with
b_{n}=\frac{1}{2}
and composable arrows
(a,\varepsilon_{i}),(a,\varepsilon_{p}),(c,\varepsilon_{r})\in\pi
, such that none of them satisfies the condition that the
n
-th component of the local state is
\frac{1}{2}
while the arrow is
\varepsilon_{0}
, vanishes. The only configuration compatible with that case is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{i}}
=\check{R}^{\alpha,0}_{0,i}(u,a),

but since
(a,\varepsilon_{i})
and
(a,\varepsilon_{0})
are inside the groupoid
\pi
and
a_{n}\neq\frac{1}{2}
, it follows that
i=-n
and
a_{n}=\frac{3}{2}
, which gives
[a_{0}-a_{-n}-1]=0
.

A similar argument shows that the second identity holds in the restricted space.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

In the
A_{n-1}^{(1)}
case, the following relations are satisfied on the restricted space:

\displaystyle\omega^{(23)}\check{R}_{VV}^{(12)}(u+\lambda)\check{R}^{(23)}_{V^{*}V}(u)

\displaystyle=\omega\otimes\rho_{2}(u),

\displaystyle\omega^{*(23)}\check{R}^{(12)}_{V^{*}V}(u+\lambda)\check{R}^{(23)}_{VV}(u)

\displaystyle=\omega^{*}\otimes\mathbb{1},

\displaystyle\check{R}^{(12)}_{V^{*}V}(u)\check{R}^{(23)}_{VV}(u+\lambda)\sigma^{(12)}

\displaystyle=\rho_{2}(u)\otimes\sigma,

\displaystyle\check{R}^{(12)}_{VV}(u)\check{R}^{(23)}_{V^{*}V}(u+\lambda)\sigma^{*(12)}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{1}\otimes\sigma^{*(23)},

where

\rho_{2}(u)=\frac{[\lambda+u][\lambda-u]}{[1+\lambda+u][1+\lambda-u]},

(35)

and
\mathbb{1}
is the
n\times n
identity matrix.

Proof.

The first relation follows from the inversion relation (
59
),

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k,o,p\\
\varepsilon_{p}-\varepsilon_{k}=-\varepsilon_{j}+\varepsilon_{m}\\
\varepsilon_{o}+\varepsilon_{k}=\varepsilon_{p}+\varepsilon_{i}\end{subarray}}\omega_{k,-l}(\check{R}_{VV})^{o,k}_{i,p}(u+\lambda)(\check{R}_{V^{*}V})^{p,-k}_{-j,m}(u)e_{o}-\omega_{i,-j}e_{m}\rho_{2}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{g}\sqrt{\frac{G_{g}}{G_{b}}}\mathcal{W}\Big(\begin{matrix}a&b\\
d&g\end{matrix}\Big|\lambda+u\Big)\sqrt{\frac{G_{c}G_{g}}{G_{b}G_{d}}}\mathcal{W}\Big(\begin{matrix}c&d\\
b&g\end{matrix}\Big|\lambda-u\Big)e_{o}-\delta_{m,o}\sqrt{\frac{G_{d}}{G_{a}}}e_{m}\rho_{2}(u)=0,

where the indices are clear from the picture,

\sum_{g}\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{G_{g}}{G_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{o}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}_{VV}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}_{V^{*}V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{m}}
=\delta_{i,j}\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{G_{d}}{G_{a}}}\rho_{2}(u).

The other equations follow from inverse relations (
58
) and (
61
) in a similar way.

To prove that relations hold on restricted space, we first recall from the proof of the Lemma
4.2
that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}_{VV}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{j}}

with
(a,\varepsilon_{i}),(d,\varepsilon_{j})\in\pi
is non-vanishing only when
(a,\varepsilon_{k}),(b,\varepsilon_{l})\in\pi
, and is finite in that case. Similarly, the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}_{V^{*}V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{j}}

=\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{G_{b}G_{d}}{G_{a}G_{c}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{j}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}_{VV}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{l}}

with
(d,\varepsilon_{i}),(d,\varepsilon_{j})\in\pi
can only have
(a,\varepsilon_{k}),(c,\varepsilon_{l})\in\pi
for the following reason: firstly, it is finite since
a,c,d\in P^{\ell+g}_{++}
means
G_{a}\neq 0
,
G_{c}\neq 0
and
[d_{j}-d_{l}]\neq 0
. Furthermore, if we assume that
b\notin P^{\ell+g}_{++}
, it holds that
b_{k-1}=b_{k}
, since
a_{k-1}>a_{k}
,
b_{k-1}=a_{k-1}
and
b_{k}=a_{k}+1
, therefore
G_{b}=0
.

From this, one can easily see with restricted parameters
a,b,c,d,g
and weights
\varepsilon_{i},\varepsilon_{j},\varepsilon_{m},\varepsilon_{o}
, in the first equation

\sum_{g}\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{G_{g}}{G_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{o}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}_{VV}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{p}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{j}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\varepsilon_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{m}}
=\delta_{i,j}\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{G_{d}}{G_{a}}}\rho_{2}(u).

all the contributions that leave the restricted space vanish. The same holds for other relations.
∎

Corollary 4.6
.

In the
A_{n-1}^{(1)}
case we have the inversion identities

\displaystyle\check{R}_{VV^{*}}(-u)\check{R}_{V^{*}V}(u)=\rho_{2}(u),\qquad\check{R}_{V^{*}V}(u)\check{R}_{VV^{*}}(-u)=\rho_{2}(u)

that hold on
(V^{*})^{\mathrm{RSOS}}\otimes V^{\mathrm{RSOS}}
and
V^{\mathrm{RSOS}}\otimes(V^{*})^{\mathrm{RSOS}}
, respectively.

Proof.

This follows from Lemma
3.11
and Lemma
4.5
.
∎

5.
Restricted quantum groups

In this section, we introduce a class of
\pi^{2}
-graded Hopf algebras based on

R
-matrices on
V\otimes V
for a
\pi
-graded vector space
V
. They are defined by generators and
relations, where the generators have a definite
\pi^{2}
-degree and the relations are homogeneous, so that
the quotient is a
\pi^{2}
-graded algebra.
The main examples are obtained
from the RSOS models of Jimbo, Miwa, and Okado associated with classical Lie
algebras of the previous section, but we
first consider a more general situation, which presumably also applies to general simple
Lie algebras. Out of such an
R
-matrix one obtains a
\pi^{2}
-graded bialgebra
defined by a graded version of RTT relations. To construct the antipode and
obtain a Hopf algebra, one needs an additional property: the crossing symmetry,
which relies on an inner product. Two cases are to be considered:
either the inner product is defined on the space
V
and we say that the corresponding quantum group is of orthogonal type (including the symplectic case); or
the inner product is defined on
V\oplus V^{*}
and we extend the
R
matrix to this space by “rotation”, and we say that the quantum group is of general linear type.

5.1.
Elliptic quantum algebras

In the general context of the quantum inverse scattering method
[
40
]
,
[
39
]

quantum algebras are defined by RTT relations which are quadratic relations, each of the form

\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{n}c_{\alpha,\beta}(u_{1}-u_{2})t_{\alpha}(u_{1})t_{\beta}(u_{2})=0

(36)

for generating functions
t_{\alpha}(u)
of generators and generating functions
c_{\alpha,\beta}(u)
of
structure constants (matrix entries of
R
-matrices). The meaning of “generating function” depends on the
type of algebra and of the analytic structure of the coefficients
c_{\alpha,\beta}(u)
. For example in
the case of quantum affine algebras
[
39
]
the coefficients are meromorphic periodic
functions of
u
admitting a power series expansion in the variable
z=\exp(2\pi iu/L)
for some period
L
.
Accordingly, the generators
t_{\alpha}[n]
of the algebra
A

are collected in a formal Fourier series
t_{\alpha}(u)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}t_{\alpha}[n]z^{n}\in A[[z]]
. Expanding (
36
) in powers of the two exponential variables
z_{1},z_{2}
and comparing coefficients
yields an infinite set of quadratic relations for the generator, each involving finitely many generators. This
can be best seen by writing (
36
) in multiplicative notation
c_{\alpha}(u)=\tilde{c}_{\alpha}(z)
,
t_{\alpha}(u)=\tilde{t}_{\alpha}(z)
:

\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{n}\tilde{c}_{\alpha,\beta}(z_{1})\tilde{t}_{\alpha}(z_{1}z_{2})\tilde{t}_{\beta}(z_{2})=0,\quad\text{in $A[[z_{1},z_{2}]]$.}

(37)

For our application to elliptic restricted quantum groups we wish to generalize this to the elliptic case. It is convenient to consider
families
of algebras with quadratic relations parametrized by a modular parameter
\tau
in the upper half-plane and include the trigonometric limit
\tau\to i\infty
. The relations take the form

\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{n}c_{\alpha,\beta}(u_{1}-u_{2},\tau)t_{\alpha}(u_{1},\tau)t_{\beta}(u_{2},\tau)=0,

(38)

where the coefficients
c_{\alpha,\beta}(u,\tau)
are elliptic functions admitting a convergent Fourier expansion

c_{\alpha,\beta}(u,\tau)=\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}c_{\alpha,\beta}[n,m]e^{2\pi inu/L}e^{2\pi im(\tau-u/L)}.

In the restricted SOS models of Section
4
, this follows from the form of the
R
-matrix coefficients listed in Section
4.1
and the Jacobi
product formula (
21
) for the theta function
[x]
of
x
and
\tau
.
Accordingly, the relations (
38
) are
relations for the coefficients
t_{\alpha}[n,m]
of generating functions

t_{\alpha}(u)=\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}t_{\alpha}[n,m]z^{n}(p/z)^{m},\quad z=e^{2\pi iu/L},\quad p=e^{2\pi i\tau}.

Expanding (
38
) and comparing coefficients yields infinitely many quadratic relations for the
generators
t_{\alpha}[n,m]
, each involving a finite subset of generators. The
t_{\alpha}[n,0]
obey
relations (
36
) for the trigonometric limit
c_{\alpha,\beta}(u,\tau=i\infty)
of the coefficients.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathcal{O}=\mathbb{C}[[z,p/z]]
. An
elliptic quantum algebra
is a unital algebra over
\mathbb{C}[p]
generated by
t_{\alpha}[n,m]
, for
\alpha
in a finite index set and
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
subject to relations of the form (
37
), with
c_{\alpha,\beta}\in\mathcal{O}
.

We can write the relations in a form generalizing (
37
) by introducing the variable
w=p/z
.
With
\tilde{c}_{\alpha,\beta}(z,w)=\sum_{n,m\geq 0}c_{\alpha,\beta}[n,m]z^{n}w^{m}
and
\tilde{t}_{\alpha}(z,w)=\sum_{n,m\geq 0}t_{\alpha}[n,m]z^{n}w^{m}
, the relations (
38
) are

\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{n}\tilde{c}_{\alpha,\beta}(z_{1},z_{2}w)\tilde{t}_{\alpha}(z_{1}z_{2},w)\tilde{t}_{\beta}(z_{2},z_{1}w)=0,\quad\text{in $A[[z_{1},z_{2},w]]$.}

The relations are bilinear over
\mathbb{C}[p]
where
p=z_{1}z_{2}w
. They reduce to the relations (
37
) in the trigonometric
specialization
w=0
. It is useful to think of the power series in
z
and
p/z
as functions on the “formal annulus”: convergent series with convergence radius 1 in both variables represent holomorphic functions on the family of annuli
|p|<|z|<1
.

5.2.
Restricted quantum groups of general linear type

Let
\pi
be a finite groupoid with at most one morphism between any two objects. Take
V\in\operatorname{Vect}^{f}_{\mathbb{C}}(\pi)
to be a finite-dimensional
\pi
-graded vector space over
\mathbb{C}
, and denote its dual space by
V^{*}
. Consider all the arrows
k\in\pi_{1}
such that
V_{k}\neq\{0\}
and let
\tilde{\pi}\subseteq\pi
be the set containing all such arrows. Clearly, all nonempty components of
V^{*}
are graded by the set
-\tilde{\pi}:=\{k^{-1}\mid k\in\tilde{\pi}\}
.

Let
\check{R}_{VV}(u)\in\operatorname{End}(V\otimes V)
be a crossing-symmetric
R
-matrix as described in Section
3.1
and let
\check{R}_{V^{*}V}(u)
,
\check{R}_{V^{*}V^{*}}(u)
and
\check{R}_{VV^{*}}(u)
be its rotations given by Definition
3.5
. These
R
-matrices will be used as coefficients for quadratic relations defining elliptic quantum algebras as in the previous section and we therefore assume that the matrix elements of
\check{R}_{VV}(u)
are meromorphic functions of
u\in\mathbb{C}
and
\tau
in the upper half-plane admitting a power series expansion in the variables
e^{2\pi iu/L},e^{2\pi i(\tau-u/L)}

Consider the double groupoid
\Gamma=\pi^{2}
and let
(A_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\pi^{2}}
be a
\pi^{2}
-graded unital algebra described in Section
2.3
with
\pi
being replaced by
\Gamma^{\circ}
. It is generated by
T_{i}^{j}[m,n]
and
(T^{*})_{-i}^{-j}[m,n]
,
i,j\in\tilde{\pi}
, compactly represented by a formal Fourier series of the form

\begin{split}T_{i}^{j}(u)=&\sum_{m,n\geq 0}T_{i}^{j}[m,n]e^{2\pi inu/L}(p\,e^{-2\pi iu/L})^{m},\\
(T^{*})_{-i}^{-j}(u)=&\sum_{m,n\geq 0}(T^{*})_{-i}^{-j}[m,n]e^{2\pi inu/L}(p\,e^{-2\pi iu/L})^{m},\end{split}

(39)

as in Section
5.1
.
The generators
T_{i}^{j}(u)
and
(T^{*})_{-i}^{-j}(u)
live in the algebra component graded by
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\bullet}(i,j)
,
\beta\in\Gamma^{\bullet}(i^{-1},j^{-1})
,

\displaystyle T^{j}_{i}(u)\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(V_{i},V_{j})\otimes A_{\alpha},\qquad(T^{*})^{-j}_{-i}(u)\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}((V^{*})_{i^{-1}},(V^{*})_{j^{-1}})\otimes A_{\beta}.

Here we really mean that the Fourier coefficients of
T_{i}^{j}(u)
and
(T^{*})^{-j}_{-i}(u)
live in
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(V_{i},V_{j})\otimes A_{\alpha}
and
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}((V^{*})_{i^{-1}},(V^{*})_{j^{-1}})\otimes A_{\beta}
, respectively.
These generators can be graphically represented by their
\Gamma
-graded component, i.e. the commutative square with the fixed vertical morphisms
i
and
j
,

T_{i}^{j}(u)=
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
,
\qquad(T^{*})_{-i}^{-j}(u)=

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{*}(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
.

We obtain the standard dynamical formulation
T^{j}_{i}(u,a)\in A_{\alpha}
by setting the dynamical parameter
a
to be the source of the left vertical arrow
i
,
a=s(i)
. The dynamical formulation of
(T^{*})^{-j}_{-i}(u)
is
(T^{*})^{-j}_{-i}(u,c)
for
c=s(i^{-1})
.

The unit
\eta\colon I_{v}\to A
has a non-vanishing component
\eta(1)\in\oplus_{f\in\pi_{1}}A_{\mathrm{id}_{f}^{\circ}}
and is presented as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta(1)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
. We will also consider central elements
\xi^{+}[m,n]
and
\xi^{-}[m,n]
, whose non-vanishing components have the same grading as the unit, and can be represented by a formal power series

\xi^{\pm}(u)=\sum_{m,n\geq 0}\xi^{\pm}[m,n]e^{2\pi inu/L}(q^{2}e^{-2\pi iu/L})^{m}.

Let us further introduce matrix
T(u)\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Vect}^{f}_{\mathbb{C}}(\pi)}(V,A\otimes_{h}V)
, where
A\otimes_{h}V
is
\pi
-graded,
(A\otimes_{h}V)_{i}=\oplus_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{\bullet}(i,j)}A_{\alpha}\otimes V_{j}
, and its matrix elements are
T^{j}_{i}(u)
. Similarly, we have
T^{*}(u)\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Vect}^{f}_{\mathbb{C}}(\pi)}(V^{*},A\otimes_{h}V^{*})
with
\pi
-graded vector space
(A\otimes_{h}V^{*})_{i^{-1}}=\oplus_{\alpha\in\Gamma^{\bullet}(i^{-1},j^{-1})}A_{\alpha}\otimes V^{*}_{j^{-1}}
and matrix elements
(T^{*})^{-j}_{-i}(u)
.

Before introducing the definition of restricted quantum group, we first need to understand the
RTT
-relations in the context of double groupoid graded algebras. We have the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V\otimes_{v}V}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(*)}

with

,

and

the morphism (

) where we used

-grading of

-grading via

.

We also have the relations of type
\omega^{(23)}T^{(1)}(u+\lambda)T^{*(2)}(u)=\omega^{(12)}
described by the diagram

given by the commutative diagram

Definition 5.2
.

Let
\check{R}_{VV},\omega,\omega^{*}
be as in Section
3.1
and let

,

,

be the rotations of

as in Definition
3.5
. Assume
that the crossing symmetry
3.9
with parameter
\lambda
holds.
The
restricted quantum algebra of general linear type
associated with these data
is the
\pi^{2}
-graded unital algebra
A=A_{R,\omega,\omega^{*}}=\oplus_{\alpha\in\pi^{2}}A_{\alpha}
generated by matrix elements
T_{i}^{j}(u)
,
(T^{*})_{-i}^{-j}(u)
,
i,j\in\tilde{\pi}
, of
T(u)\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}(\pi)}(V,A\otimes_{h}V)
and central elements
\xi_{2}^{+}(u),\xi_{2}^{-}(u)\in\oplus_{f\in\pi_{1}}A_{\mathrm{id}_{f}^{\circ}}
, subject to relations

\xi_{2}^{\pm}(u)\xi_{2}^{\mp}(u)=\eta(1),

(40)

\check{R}_{VV}^{(23)}(u_{1}-u_{2})T^{(1)}(u_{1})T^{(2)}(u_{2})=T^{(1)}(u_{2})T^{(2)}(u_{1})\check{R}_{VV}^{(12)}(u_{1}-u_{2}),

(41)

\begin{split}\check{R}_{V^{*}V^{*}}^{(23)}(u_{1}-u_{2})T^{*(1)}(u_{1})T^{*(2)}(u_{2})=T^{*(1)}(u_{2})T^{*(2)}(u_{1})\check{R}_{V^{*}V^{*}}^{(12)}(u_{1}-u_{2}),\end{split}

(42)

\begin{split}\check{R}_{V^{*}V}^{(23)}(u_{1}-u_{2})T^{*(1)}(u_{1})T^{(2)}(u_{2})=T^{(1)}(u_{2})T^{*(2)}(u_{1})\check{R}_{V^{*}V}^{(12)}(u_{1}-u_{2}),\end{split}

(43)

\omega^{(23)}T^{(1)}(u+\lambda)T^{*(2)}(u)=\omega^{(12)}\xi_{2}^{+}(u),

(44)

\omega^{*(23)}T^{*(1)}(u+\lambda)T^{(2)}(u)=\omega^{*(12)},

(45)

(46)

where

,

and

are maps defined in Section

.

Remark 5.3
.

As explained in Section
5.1
, expanding
T_{i}^{j}(u)
,
(T^{*})_{i}^{j}(u)
as formal power series (
39
), (
41
)-(
47
) become relations involving only finitely many terms
T_{i}^{j}[m_{1},n_{1}]T_{k}^{l}[m_{2},n_{2}]
. The same holds for central elements
\xi_{2}^{\pm}(u)
.

It is useful to have graphical representations of the relations above, for example relations (
41
), (
44
) and (
46
) become

\sum_{k,l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(u_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(u_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
=\sum_{k,l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(u_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(u_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
,

where the side diagrams represent
(\check{R}_{VV})^{m,n}_{k,l}(u_{1}-u_{2})
and
(\check{R}_{VV})^{k,l}_{i,j}(u_{1}-u_{2})
, respectively, and

\sum_{k,l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{*}(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
=\xi_{2}^{+}(u)\omega_{i,j},\quad

\sum_{k,l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{*}(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
=\xi_{2}^{+}(u)\sigma^{i,j},

with
v=u+\lambda
.

We define two maps, the co-unit
\epsilon:A_{R,\omega,\omega^{*}}\rightarrow I_{h}
and the coproduct
\Delta:A_{R,\omega,\omega^{*}}\rightarrow A_{R,\omega,\omega^{*}}\otimes_{h}A_{R,\omega,\omega^{*}}
by

\displaystyle\epsilon(T_{\mathrm{id}^{\bullet}_{i}}(u)):=1,\qquad\epsilon((T^{*})_{\mathrm{id}^{\bullet}_{i^{-1}}}(u)):=1,

and

\displaystyle\Delta(T^{j}_{i}(u))

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k}T^{k}_{i}(u)\otimes_{h}T^{j}_{k}(u),

\displaystyle\Delta((T^{*})^{-j}_{-i}(u))

\displaystyle:=\sum_{l}(T^{*})^{-l}_{-i}(u)\otimes_{h}(T^{*})^{-j}_{-l}(u),

where
k
goes over all morphisms such that
t^{\circ}(\beta)\circ t^{\circ}(\alpha)=t^{\circ}(\gamma)
for
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\bullet}(i,k)
,
\beta\in\Gamma^{\bullet}(k,j)
,
\gamma\in\Gamma^{\bullet}(i,j)
in the first relation and
l
goes over all morphisms such that
t^{\circ}(\beta^{\prime})\circ t^{\circ}(\alpha^{\prime})=t^{\circ}(\gamma^{\prime})
for
\alpha^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\bullet}(i^{-1},l^{-1})
,
\beta^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\bullet}(l^{-1},j^{-1})
,
\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{\bullet}(i^{-1},j^{-1})
in the second relation.
We extend the definitions on generators multiplicatively by declaring that
\Delta,\epsilon
are algebra homomorphisms.
Note that for the co-unit one can write

\displaystyle\epsilon(T^{j}_{i}(u))=\delta_{i,j},\qquad\epsilon((T^{*})^{-j}_{-i}(u))=\delta_{i,j}.

The graphical representation of the action of coproduct is

\epsilon\Bigg(
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\Bigg)=\delta_{i,j},\qquad\epsilon\Bigg(
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{*}(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{-1}}
\Bigg)=\delta_{i,j}
,

\Delta\Bigg(
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\Bigg)=\sum_{k,o\circ n=m}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quad T(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o}
,

and similarly for
\Delta(T^{*}(u))
. For the central elements
\xi_{2}^{\pm}(u)
one has

\epsilon\Bigg(
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{2}^{\pm}(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{b}}
\Bigg):=\delta_{a,b},

\Delta\Bigg(
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{2}^{\pm}(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\Bigg):=\sum_{c,o\circ n=m}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{a}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{2}^{\pm}(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\quad\xi_{2}^{\pm}(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces o}

and similar for the unit element
\eta(1)
.

Remark 5.4
.

Strictly speaking, the definition of the co-unit and the coproduct is as follows:

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}\otimes\epsilon)(T_{i}^{j}(u))=\operatorname{id}_{V_{i}}\delta_{i,j},\qquad(\operatorname{id}\otimes\Delta)(T_{i}^{j}(u))=m^{(13)}\sum_{k}T_{i}^{k}(u)\otimes T_{k}^{j}(u),

and similarly for
(T^{*})_{i}^{j}(u)
. However, we will use the abbreviated form to avoid cumbersome expressions in proofs.

Lemma 5.5
.

The co-unit
\epsilon
and the coproduct
\Delta
preserve relations (
41
) - (
47
) and satisfy the coalgebra axioms.

Proof.

We show that the statement holds for the relations (
41
) and (
44
). The proof for other relations follows in an analogous way.

The action of the co-unit on the left-hand side of (
41
) gives

\epsilon\Bigg(\sum_{k,l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(u_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(u_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
)
=\sum_{k,l}\delta_{i,k}\delta_{j,l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}_{VV}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
=\delta_{a,b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}_{VV}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

This is equal to the right-hand side,

\epsilon\Bigg(\sum_{k,l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(u_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(u_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\Bigg)=\sum_{k,l}\delta_{k,m}\delta_{l,n}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}_{VV}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
=\delta_{a,b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{R}_{VV}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

The same holds for the identity (
44
),

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l}\omega_{k,-l}\epsilon\left(T^{k}_{i}(u+\lambda)\right)\epsilon\left((T^{*})_{-j}^{-l}(u)\right)-\omega_{i,-j}\epsilon\left(\xi_{2}^{+}(u)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l}\omega_{k,-l}\delta_{i,k}\delta_{j,l}-\omega_{i,-j}=0.

The coproduct is also compatible with the
RTT
-relations:

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l}(\check{R}_{VV})^{m,n}_{k,l}(u_{1}-u_{2})\Delta(T^{k}_{i}(u_{1}))\Delta(T^{l}_{j}(u_{2}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l,o,p}T^{o}_{i}(u_{1})T^{p}_{j}(u_{2})\otimes_{h}(\check{R}_{VV})^{m,n}_{k,l}(u_{1}-u_{2})T^{k}_{o}(u_{1})T^{l}_{p}(u_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l,o,p}(\check{R}_{VV})^{k,l}_{o,p}(u_{1}-u_{2})T^{o}_{i}(u_{1})T^{p}_{j}(u_{2})\otimes_{h}T^{m}_{k}(u_{2})T^{n}_{l}(u_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l,o,p}T^{k}_{o}(u_{2})T^{l}_{p}(u_{1})(\check{R}_{VV})^{o,p}_{i,j}(u_{1}-u_{2})\otimes_{h}T^{m}_{k}(u_{2})T^{n}_{l}(u_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{o,p}\Delta(T^{m}_{o}(u_{2}))\Delta(T^{n}_{p}(u_{1}))(\check{R}_{VV})^{o,p}_{i,j}(u_{1}-u_{2}),

and with the identity (
44
):

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l}\omega_{k,-l}\Delta(T^{k}_{i}(u+\lambda))\Delta((T^{*})^{-l}_{-j}(u))

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l,o,p}\omega_{k,-l}T^{o}_{i}(u+\lambda)(T^{*})^{-p}_{-j}(u)\otimes_{h}T^{k}_{o}(u+\lambda)(T^{*})^{-l}_{-p}(u)

\displaystyle=\xi_{2}^{+}(u)\otimes\sum_{o,p}\omega_{o,-p}T^{o}_{i}(u+\lambda)(T^{*})^{-p}_{-j}(u)=\omega_{i,-j}\xi_{2}^{+}(u)\otimes_{h}\xi_{2}^{+}(u)=\omega_{i,-j}\Delta(\xi_{2}^{+}(u)).

Finally, we prove that the coalgebra axioms are satisfied:

(1)

(\operatorname{id}\otimes_{h}\Delta)\circ\Delta=(\Delta\otimes_{h}\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta
:

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}\otimes_{h}\Delta)\circ\Delta\left(T^{j}_{i}(u)\right)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}\otimes_{h}\Delta)\sum_{k}T^{k}_{i}(u)\otimes_{h}T^{j}_{k}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}T^{k}_{i}(u)\otimes_{h}\sum_{l}T^{l}_{k}(u)\otimes_{h}T^{j}_{l}(u)

\displaystyle=(\Delta\otimes_{h}\operatorname{id})\sum_{l}T^{l}_{i}(u)\otimes_{h}T^{j}_{l}(u)

\displaystyle=(\Delta\otimes_{h}\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta\left(T^{j}_{i}(u)\right),

(2)

(\operatorname{id}\otimes_{h}\epsilon)\circ\Delta=(\epsilon\otimes_{h}\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta=\operatorname{id}
:

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}\otimes_{h}\epsilon)\circ\Delta\left(T^{j}_{i}(u)\right)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{id}\otimes_{h}\epsilon)\sum_{k}T^{k}_{i}(u)\otimes_{h}T^{j}_{k}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}T^{k}_{i}(u)\delta_{k,j}=T^{j}_{i}(u)

\displaystyle(\epsilon\otimes_{h}\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta\left(T^{j}_{i}(u)\right)

\displaystyle=(\epsilon\otimes_{h}\operatorname{id})\sum_{k}T^{k}_{i}(u)\otimes_{h}T^{j}_{k}(u)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\delta_{i,k}T^{j}_{k}(u)=T^{j}_{i}(u),

and similarly for the
(T^{*})^{-i}_{-j}(u)
type of generator and
\alpha(u)
.
∎

Since these maps respect the defining relations, they descend to well-defined maps on
A_{R,\omega,\omega^{*}}
.

Define further the antipode
S:A_{R,\omega,\omega^{*}}\rightarrow A_{R,\omega,\omega^{*}}^{\textrm{op}}
as

\displaystyle S(T^{j}_{i}(u))

\displaystyle:=\xi_{2}^{-}(u-\lambda)\omega_{i,-i}(T^{*})_{-j}^{-i}(u-\lambda)\sigma^{-j,j},

\displaystyle S((T^{*})_{-i}^{-j}(u))

\displaystyle:=\omega_{-i,i}^{*}T^{i}_{j}(u-\lambda)\sigma^{*j,-j},

(48)

\displaystyle S(\xi_{2}^{\pm}(u))

\displaystyle:=\xi_{2}^{\mp}(u),

\displaystyle S(\eta(1))

\displaystyle:=\eta(1).

Graphically, it is given by

S\Bigg(

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\Bigg)=\displaystyle\xi_{2}^{-}(u-\lambda)\omega_{i,-i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{*}(v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{-1}}
\sigma^{-j,j}
,

S\Bigg(
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{*}(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{-1}}
\Bigg)=\displaystyle\omega_{-i,i}^{*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\sigma^{*j,-j}
,

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k^{-1}}
,

with
v=u-\lambda
.

Remark 5.6
.

The precise definition of antipode is

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}\otimes S)(T_{i}^{j}(u))

\displaystyle:=\xi_{2}^{-}(u-\lambda)\omega_{i,-i}(T^{*})_{-j}^{-i}(u-\lambda)\sigma^{-j,j},

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}\otimes S)((T^{*})_{-i}^{-j}(u))

\displaystyle:=\omega_{-i,i}^{*}T^{i}_{j}(u-\lambda)\sigma^{*j,-j},

but we adopt the simplified notation for brevity.

Lemma 5.7
.

The antipode
S
satisfies the antipode axioms and preserves relations (
41
)-(
47
).

Proof.

The antipode axioms (H7) are described precisely by the identities (
44
)-(
47
). To see this, first note that the projection map
A\otimes A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\twoheadrightarrow}}A\otimes_{v}A
sends

T^{j}_{i}(u)\otimes T^{l}_{k}(v)\mapsto\delta_{t^{\circ}(\alpha_{ij}),s^{\circ}(\alpha_{kl})}T^{j}_{i}(u)\otimes_{v}T^{l}_{k}(v),

where
\alpha_{ij}
denotes a morphism
\alpha\in\pi^{2}
with
s^{\bullet}=i
and
t^{\bullet}=j
, as in the picture:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}}

\otimes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{2}}
\mapsto\delta_{m_{1},n_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}}
\otimes_{v}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{2}}
,

which upon the action of the product
\nabla
becomes
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}}

\otimes_{v}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{2}}
\mapsto

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{2}}
.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\nabla\circ p\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes S)\circ i\circ\Delta\left(T^{j}_{i}(u)\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla\circ p\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes S)\left(\sum_{k}T^{k}_{i}(u)\otimes T^{j}_{k}(u)\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla\circ p\left(\sum_{k}T^{k}_{i}(u)\otimes\xi_{2}^{-}(u-\lambda)\omega_{k,-k}(T^{*})^{-k}_{-j}(u-\lambda)\sigma^{-j,j}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta_{t^{\circ}(\alpha_{i,k}),s^{\circ}(\alpha_{j^{-1},k^{-1}})}\xi_{2}^{-}(u-\lambda)\sum_{k}\omega_{k,-k}T^{k}_{i}(u)(T^{*})^{-k}_{-j}(u-\lambda)\sigma^{-j,j}

\displaystyle=\omega_{i,-j}\sigma^{-j,j}\xi_{2}^{-}(u-\lambda)\xi_{2}^{+}(u-\lambda)=\delta_{i,j}\eta(1)=\eta\circ\sigma^{\prime}\circ\epsilon\left(T^{j}_{i}(u)\right)

and

\displaystyle\nabla\circ p\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes S)\circ i\circ\Delta\left((T^{*})^{-j}_{-i}(u)\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla\circ p\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes S)\left(\sum_{k}(T^{*})^{-k}_{-i}(u)\otimes(T^{*})^{-j}_{-k}(u)\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla\circ p\left(\sum_{k}(T^{*})^{-k}_{-i}(u)\otimes\omega^{*}_{-k,k}T^{k}_{j}(u-\lambda)\sigma^{*j,-j}\right)

\displaystyle=\delta_{t^{\circ}(\alpha_{i^{-1},k^{-1}}),s^{\circ}(\alpha_{j,k})}\sum_{k}\omega^{*}_{-k,k}(T^{*})^{-k}_{-i}(u)T^{k}_{j}(u-\lambda)\sigma^{*j,-j}

\displaystyle=\omega^{*}_{-i,j}\sigma^{*j,-j}\eta(1)=\delta_{i,j}\eta(1)=\eta\circ\sigma^{\prime}\circ\epsilon\left((T^{*})^{-j}_{-i}(u)\right).

Moreover, we have
\nabla\circ p\circ(\operatorname{id}\otimes S)\circ i\circ\Delta(\xi_{2}^{\pm}(u))=\xi_{2}^{\pm}(u)\xi_{2}^{\mp}(u)=\eta(1)=\eta\circ\sigma^{\prime}\circ\epsilon(\xi_{2}^{\pm}(u))
.
The other identity follows in a similar way. From these axioms one can in a standard way prove that the map
S
is an antihomomorphism.

Furthermore, we show that the antipode preserves the
RTT
-relations. To do so, we first observe that from the rotation relations (
16
) together with
\sigma^{-i,i}\omega_{i,-i}=1
and follow the identities

\displaystyle\sigma^{-l,l}\sigma^{-k,k}(\check{R}_{VV})_{k,l}^{m,n}(u)

\displaystyle=(\check{R}_{V^{*}V^{*}})_{-n,-m}^{-l,-k}(u)\sigma^{-m,m}\sigma^{-n,n},

\displaystyle(\check{R}_{VV})_{k,l}^{m,n}(u)\omega_{n,-n}\omega_{m,-m}

\displaystyle=\omega_{k,-k}\omega_{l,-l}(\check{R}_{V^{*}V^{*}})_{-n,-m}^{-l,-k}(u).

Now we apply the antipode on the relation (
41
):

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l}(\check{R}_{VV})^{m,n}_{k,l}(u_{1}-u_{2})S\left(T^{k}_{i}(u_{1})T^{l}_{j}(u_{2})\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{k,l}S\left(T^{m}_{k}(u_{2})T^{n}_{l}(u_{1})\right)(\check{R}_{VV})^{k,l}_{i,j}(u_{1}-u_{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l}\omega_{j,-j}(T^{*})^{-j}_{-l}(v_{2})\sigma^{-l,l}\omega_{i,-i}(T^{*})^{-i}_{-k}(v_{1})\sigma^{-k,k}(\check{R}_{VV})^{m,n}_{k,l}(v_{1}-v_{2})

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{k,l}(\check{R}_{VV})^{k,l}_{i,j}(v_{1}-v_{2})\omega_{l,-l}(T^{*})^{-l}_{-n}(v_{1})\sigma^{-n,n}\omega_{k,-k}(T^{*})^{-k}_{-m}(v_{2})\sigma^{-m,m}

\displaystyle=\omega_{i,-i}\omega_{j,-j}\Bigg(\sum_{k,l}(T^{*})^{-j}_{-l}(v_{2})(T^{*})^{-i}_{-k}(v_{1})(\check{R}_{V^{*}V^{*}})^{-l,-k}_{-n,-m}(v_{1}-v_{2})

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{k,l}(\check{R}_{V^{*}V^{*}})^{-j,-i}_{-l,-k}(v_{1}-v_{2})(T^{*})^{-l}_{-n}(v_{1})(T^{*})^{-k}_{-m}(v_{2})\Bigg)\sigma^{-m,m}\sigma^{-n,n}=0,

where we set
v_{i}:=u_{i}-\lambda
,
i=1,2
and we use the relation (
42
).

On the other hand, using the definition of
\check{R}_{V^{*}V^{*}}^{\prime}(u)
and Lemma
3.6
, one can see that the action of antipode on the relation (
42
) gives back the relation (
41
).

To show that the relation (
43
) is preserved upon the action of antipode, we first observe that due to Corollary
3.7
, we have the following identities

\displaystyle\sigma^{*k,-k}\sigma^{-l,l}(\check{R}_{VV^{*}})_{l,-k}^{-j,i}(u)

\displaystyle=(\check{R}_{V^{*}V})_{-i,j}^{k,-l}(u)\sigma^{*j,-j}\sigma^{-i,i},

\displaystyle(\check{R}_{VV^{*}})_{l,-k}^{-j,i}(u)\omega_{i,-i}\omega^{*}_{-j,j}

\displaystyle=\omega_{l,-l}\omega^{*}_{-k,k}(\check{R}_{V^{*}V})_{-i,j}^{k,-l}(u),

\displaystyle\sigma^{-j,j}\sigma^{*i,-i}(\check{R}_{V^{*}V})_{-i,j}^{k,-l}(u)

\displaystyle=(\check{R}_{VV^{*}})_{l,-k}^{-j,i}(u)\sigma^{-k,k}\sigma^{*l,-l},

\displaystyle(\check{R}_{V^{*}V})_{-i,j}^{k,-l}(u)\omega^{*}_{-l,l}\omega_{k,-k}

\displaystyle=\omega^{*}_{-i,i}\omega_{j,-j}(\check{R}_{VV^{*}})_{l,-k}^{-j,i}(u).

Now, since
\check{R}_{VV}(u)
is quasi-unitary, we have the inversion identity
\check{R}_{VV^{*}}(u)=\alpha(u)\check{R}_{V^{*}V}^{-1}(-u)
due to Lemma
3.11
, and we can write the relation (
43
) in the following form:

\begin{split}\check{R}_{VV^{*}}^{(23)}(u_{1}-u_{2})T^{(12)}(u_{1})T^{*(23)}(u_{2})&=T^{*(12)}(u_{2})T^{(23)}(u_{1})\check{R}_{VV^{*}}^{(12)}(u_{1}-u_{2}),\end{split}

which can be written as

\begin{split}\sum_{k,l}(\check{R}_{VV^{*}})^{-m,n}_{k,-l}(u_{1}-u_{2})T^{k}_{i}(u_{1})(T^{*})^{-l}_{-j}(u_{2})\\
=\sum_{k,l}(T^{*})^{-m}_{-k}(u_{2})T^{n}_{l}(u_{1})(\check{R}_{VV^{*}})^{-k,l}_{i,-j}(u_{1}-u_{2}).\end{split}

The action of the antipode on this relation with the help of the above identities for
\check{R}_{VV^{*}}(u)
and
\check{R}_{V^{*}V}(u)
gives the relation (
43
) and vice versa.

Next, we prove that the identities (
44
)–(
47
) are preserved. Applying the antipode on the left-hand side of equation (
44
) gives

\displaystyle\sum_{kl}\omega_{k,-l}S\left(T^{k}_{i}(u+\lambda)(T^{*})_{-j}^{-l}(u)\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{kl}\omega^{*}_{-j,j}T^{j}_{l}(u-\lambda)\sigma^{*l,-l}\xi_{2}^{-}(u)\omega_{i,-i}(T^{*})_{-k}^{-i}(u)\omega_{k,-l}\sigma^{-k,k}

\displaystyle=\omega_{i,-i}\omega^{*}_{-j,j}\sum_{kl}T^{j}_{l}(u-\lambda)(T^{*})_{-k}^{-i}(u)\sigma^{*l,-l}\delta_{k,l}\xi_{2}^{-}(u)

\displaystyle=\omega_{i,-i}\omega^{*}_{-j,j}\sigma^{*j,-i}\xi_{2}^{-}(u)=\omega_{i,-j}S(\xi_{2}^{+}(u)).

It is useful to have a graphical understanding of this proof:

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l^{-1}}

\xrightarrow{S}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

Now we can contract
\omega_{k,-l}\sigma^{-k,k}=\delta_{k,l}
, which further allows the action of
\sigma^{*l,-l}
:

,

where the equality comes from relation (

). The left diagram on the right-hand side corresponds to contraction

which together with the right diagram gives

=\omega_{i,-j}
.

The proof for other relations follows in an analogous way.
∎

Theorem 5.8
.

The restricted quantum algebra
A_{R,\omega,\omega^{*}}
equipped with
(\Delta,\nabla,\epsilon,\eta,S)
is a double groupoid graded Hopf algebra.

Proof.

Bialgebra axioms are satisfied by construction. This, together with Lemma
5.5
and Lemma
5.7
proves the theorem.
∎

Definition 5.9
.

We call the
\pi^{2}
-graded Hopf algebra
(A_{R_{VV},\omega,\omega^{*}},\Delta,\nabla,\epsilon,\eta,S)
the
restricted quantum group of general linear type
associated with
\check{R}_{VV}(u)
,
\omega,\omega^{*}
.

Our main example is given by the
R
-matrix of the restricted
A_{n-1}
-model of Section
4
. The rotations are
introduced in Section
4.3
and the crossing symmetry is the content of Lemma
4.5
with
\lambda=-n/2
.

Definition 5.10
.

Let
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
n\geq 2
,
L=\ell+n
,
\lambda=-n/2
,
and
\check{R}_{VV}
be the
R
-matrix of the restricted
A_{n-1}^{(1)}
-model of
Section
4
with
\omega,\omega^{*}
given by (
28
). The
\pi^{2}
-graded Hopf algebra associated
with this data is called restricted elliptic quantum group
E_{\ell}(A_{n-1}^{(1)})
of type
A_{n-1}^{(1)}
of level
\ell
.

Remark 5.11
.

For this algebra one can construct, as in
[
19
]
,
a quantum determinant which is central and group-like. This will be discussed elsewhere.

5.3.
Restricted quantum groups of orthogonal type

Let
V\in\operatorname{Vect}^{f}_{\mathbb{C}}(\pi)
be a finite dimensional
\pi
-graded vector space and let
\tilde{\pi}\subseteq\pi
denote the set that contains all the arrows
k\in\pi_{1}
with
V_{k}\neq\{0\}
and their inverses
k^{-1}\in\pi_{1}.
Consider the generators
T^{i}_{j}(u)\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}}(V_{j},V_{i})\otimes A_{\alpha}
described in the Section
5.2
.

Definition 5.12
.

Let
\check{R}(u)\in\operatorname{End}(V\otimes V)
be a rotationally invariant
R
-matrix on the
\pi
-graded vector space
V
with inner product
\omega
and crossing parameter
\lambda
.
The
restricted quantum algebra

A_{R,\omega}

of orthogonal type
associated with
R
,
\omega

is the
\pi^{2}
-graded unital algebra
A=\oplus_{\alpha\in\pi^{2}}A_{\alpha}
generated by matrix elements
T^{i}_{j}(u)
,

i,j\in\tilde{\pi}
, of
T(u)\in\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Vect}_{\mathbb{C}}(\pi)}(V,A\otimes_{h}V)
and central elements
\xi^{+}(u),\xi^{-}(u)\in\oplus_{f\in\pi_{1}}A_{\mathrm{id}_{f}^{\circ}}
,
subject to relations

\xi^{\pm}(u)\xi^{\mp}(u)=\eta(1),

(49)

\check{R}^{(23)}(u_{1}-u_{2})T^{(1)}(u_{1})T^{(2)}(u_{2})=T^{(1)}(u_{2})T^{(2)}(u_{1})\check{R}^{(12)}(u_{1}-u_{2}),

(50)

\omega^{(23)}T^{(1)}(u+\lambda)T^{(2)}(u)=\xi^{+}(u)\omega^{(12)},

(51)

T^{(1)}(u)T^{(2)}(u+\lambda)\sigma^{(12)}=\xi^{+}(u)\sigma^{(23)},

(52)

We define two maps, the co-unit
\epsilon:A_{R,\omega}\rightarrow I_{h}
and the coproduct
\Delta:A_{R,\omega}\rightarrow A_{R,\omega}\otimes_{h}A_{R,\omega}
by

\displaystyle\epsilon(T_{\textrm{id}^{\bullet}_{i}}(u)):=1,\qquad\epsilon(\xi^{\pm}(u)):=1,

and

\displaystyle\Delta(T^{i}_{j}(u)):=\sum_{k}T^{k}_{j}(u)\otimes_{h}T^{i}_{k}(u),\qquad\Delta(\xi^{\pm}(u)):=\xi^{\pm}(u)\otimes_{h}\xi^{\pm}(u),

where
k
goes over all morphisms such that
t^{\circ}(\beta)\circ t^{\circ}(\alpha)=t^{\circ}(\gamma)
for
\alpha\in\Gamma^{\bullet}(i,k)
,
\beta\in\Gamma^{\bullet}(k,j)
,
\gamma\in\Gamma^{\bullet}(i,j)
.

Lemma 5.13
.

The co-unit
\epsilon
and the coproduct
\Delta
preserve relations (
50
), (
51
), (
52
) and satisfy the coalgebra axioms.

Proof.

The proof is the same as in the case of the restricted quantum algebra
A_{R,\omega,\omega^{*}}
, which makes co-unit and coproduct well-defined algebra maps on
A_{R,\omega}
.
∎

Define the antipode
S:A_{R,\omega}\rightarrow A_{R,\omega}^{\textrm{op}}
to be

\displaystyle S(T^{j}_{i}(u))

\displaystyle:=\xi^{-}(u-\lambda)\omega_{i,-i}T_{-j}^{-i}(u-\lambda)\sigma^{-j,j},

(53)

\displaystyle S(\xi^{\pm}(u))

\displaystyle:=\xi^{\mp}(u)

whose components can be depicted as

S\Bigg(
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(u)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\Bigg)=\displaystyle\xi^{-}(u-\lambda)\omega_{i,-i}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T(v)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces n^{-1}}
\sigma^{-j,j}
,
v=u-\lambda
.

Lemma 5.14
.

The antipode
S
satisfies the antipode axioms and preserves the relations (
50
), (
51
), and (
52
).

Proof.

The identities (
51
) and (
52
) describe the antipode axioms (H7)
\nabla(\textrm{id}\otimes S)\Delta=\eta\circ\sigma^{\prime}\circ\epsilon
and
\nabla(S\otimes\textrm{id})\Delta=\eta\circ\sigma^{\prime}\circ\epsilon
in the same way as in the previous subsection. Thus, the map
S
is an antihomomorphism. Moreover, using the rotational invariance of
R
-matrix, given by Definition
3.3
, one can show that the antipode respects the defining relation (
50
). It also preserves relations (
51
) and (
52
), with the same proof as in the case of the restricted quantum group of general linear type.
∎

Theorem 5.15
.

The restricted quantum algebra
A_{R,\omega}
equipped with
(\Delta,\nabla,\epsilon,\eta,S)
is a double groupoid graded Hopf algebra.

Proof.

Lemma
5.13
and Lemma
5.14
prove the theorem.
∎

Definition 5.16
.

We call the
\pi^{2}
-graded Hopf algebra
(A_{R,\omega},\Delta,\nabla,\epsilon,\eta,S)
the
restricted quantum group of orthogonal type
associated with
\check{R}(u)
and
\omega
.

The main example is given by the
R
-matrices of the restricted
B_{n}^{(1)},C_{n}^{(1)},D_{n}^{(1)}
-models of Section
4
. The rotations are
introduced in Section
4.3
and the crossing symmetry is the content of Lemma
4.4
with
\lambda
given in Table
1
.

Definition 5.17
.

Let
\mathfrak{g}=B_{n},C_{n}
or
D_{n}
,
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
L=\frac{1}{2}(\theta,\theta)(\ell+g)
,
\lambda=-g(\theta,\theta)/4
as in Table
1
,

\check{R}
be the
R
-matrix of the restricted models of
type
\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}
of Section
4
with
\omega
given in (
27
). The
\pi^{2}
-graded Hopf algebra associated
with these data is called the restricted elliptic quantum group
E_{\ell}(\mathfrak{g}^{(1)})
of type
\mathfrak{g}^{(1)}
of level
\ell
.

5.4.
Automorphisms and the square of the antipode

We describe three classes of automorphisms of our
\pi^{2}
-graded Hopf algebras. The first two act trivially
on the central elements
\xi^{\pm}(u),\xi_{2}^{\pm}(u)
and the square of the antipode is a composition of these.

The first class comes from the grading and applies to any
\pi^{2}
-graded Hopf algebra
A
.
We have an automorphism
D_{\gamma}
for each morphism of groupoids
\gamma\colon\pi\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, where the group
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
is regarded as a groupoid with one object. Thus
\gamma
is a map
\pi_{1}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
such
that
\gamma(i\circ j)=\gamma(i)\gamma(j)
for composable arrows
i,j
and
\gamma(e)=1
for identity arrows
e
.
For example
for each map
g\colon\pi_{0}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
,
\gamma(i)=g(t(i))/g(s(i))
is such a morphism.

Lemma 5.18
.

Let
A
be a
\pi
-graded Hopf algebra and
\gamma\colon\pi\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
a morphism of groupoids.
Then the map
D_{\gamma}
acting as multiplication by
\gamma(s^{\bullet}(\alpha))/\gamma(t^{\bullet}(\alpha))
on
A_{\alpha}
,

\alpha\in\Gamma^{\bullet}
(see Section
2.4
) is a Hopf algebra automorphism.

Proof.

The product is a map
A_{\alpha}\otimes_{v}A_{\beta}\to A_{\beta\circ\alpha}
and
s^{\bullet}(\beta\circ\alpha)=s^{\bullet}(\beta)\circ s^{\bullet}(\alpha)
. Therefore
\gamma(s^{\bullet}(\beta\circ\alpha)=\gamma(s^{\bullet}(\beta))\gamma(s^{\bullet}(\alpha))
. The same holds for the target, so that

D_{\gamma}(a\cdot b)=D_{\gamma}(a)\cdot D_{\gamma}(b)
for any
a\in A_{\alpha}
,
b\in A_{\beta}
. The
coproduct is a map
\Delta\colon A_{\alpha}\to\oplus_{\beta_{2}\bullet\beta_{1}=\alpha}A_{\beta_{1}}\otimes_{h}A_{\beta_{2}}
.
Since for each term in the direct sum

s^{\bullet}(\beta_{1}))=s^{\bullet}(\alpha)
,
t^{\bullet}(\beta_{2})=t^{\bullet}(\alpha)
and
t^{\bullet}(\beta_{1})=s^{\bullet}(\beta_{2})
,
the middle term cancels and
\Delta\circ D_{\gamma}(a)=D_{\gamma}\otimes D_{\gamma}\circ\Delta(a)
for any
a\in A_{\alpha}
.
Clearly
D_{\gamma}
preserves the unit and the co-unit. The map
D_{\gamma}
also commutes with the
antipode since
S
maps
A_{\alpha}
to
A_{\iota(\alpha)}

and
s^{\bullet}(\iota(\alpha))=(t^{\bullet})^{-1}(\alpha)
,
t^{\bullet}(\iota(\alpha))=(s^{\bullet})^{-1}(\alpha)
,
see (
6
).
∎

In particular, if
\gamma(i)=g(t(i))/g(s(i))
for some function
g\colon\pi_{0}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, then

D_{\gamma}(x)=\frac{g(c)g(b)}{g(a)g(d)}x,\text{ for $x\in A_{\alpha}$, where }\alpha=\hbox to71.71pt{\vbox to51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 35.85641pt\lower-26.4708pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.40915pt}{-24.31802pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
